def get_divisors(x):
    if x == 1:
        return []
    factors = {}
    temp = x
    i = 2
    while i * i <= temp:
        while temp % i == 0:
            factors[i] = factors.get(i, 0) + 1
            temp //= i
        i += 1
    if temp > 1:
        factors[temp] = 1
    divisors = [1]
    for p in factors:
        exponents = [p**e for e in range(1, factors[p]+1)]
        new_divisors = []
        for d in divisors:
            for exp in exponents:
                new_divisors.append(d * exp)
        divisors += new_divisors
    divisors = list(set(divisors))
    if 1 in divisors:
        divisors.remove(1)
    return divisors

n = int(input())
A = [int(input()) for _ in range(n)]

all_divisors = set()
for a in A:
    if a == 1:
        continue
    divisors = get_divisors(a)
    all_divisors.update(divisors)

max_sum = 0
for k in all_divisors:
    current_sum = sum(num for num in A if num % k == 0)
    if current_sum > max_sum:
        max_sum = current_sum

print(max_sum)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.
Context: financial assurance we must provide financial assurance to governmental agencies and a variety of other entities under applicable environmental regulations relating to our landfill operations for capping , closure and post-closure costs , and related to our performance under certain collection , landfill and transfer station contracts . we satisfy these financial assurance requirements by providing surety bonds , letters of credit , or insurance policies ( financial assurance instruments ) , or trust deposits , which are included in restricted cash and marketable securities and other assets in our consolidated balance sheets . the amount of the financial assurance requirements for capping , closure and post-closure costs is determined by applicable state environmental regulations . the financial assurance requirements for capping , closure and post-closure costs may be associated with a portion of the landfill or the entire landfill . generally , states require a third-party engineering specialist to determine the estimated capping , closure and post-closure costs that are used to determine the required amount of financial assurance for a landfill . the amount of financial assurance required can , and generally will , differ from the obligation determined and recorded under u.s . gaap . the amount of the financial assurance requirements related to contract performance varies by contract . additionally , we must provide financial assurance for our insurance program and collateral for certain performance obligations . we do not expect a material increase in financial assurance requirements during 2016 , although the mix of financial assurance instruments may change . these financial assurance instruments are issued in the normal course of business and are not considered indebtedness . because we currently have no liability for the financial assurance instruments , they are not reflected in our consolidated balance sheets ; however , we record capping , closure and post-closure liabilities and insurance liabilities as they are incurred . off-balance sheet arrangements we have no off-balance sheet debt or similar obligations , other than operating leases and financial assurances , which are not classified as debt . we have no transactions or obligations with related parties that are not disclosed , consolidated into or reflected in our reported financial position or results of operations . we have not guaranteed any third-party debt . free cash flow we define free cash flow , which is not a measure determined in accordance with u.s . gaap , as cash provided by operating activities less purchases of property and equipment , plus proceeds from sales of property and equipment , as presented in our consolidated statements of cash flows . the following table calculates our free cash flow for the years ended december 31 , 2015 , 2014 and 2013 ( in millions of dollars ) : .

Question: in 2015 what was the ratio of the cash provided by operating activities to the purchases of property and equipment
Answer:

Alright, let's figure out what we need here. We want to find the ratio of cash provided by operating activities to the purchases of property and equipment for 2015. So, first things first, we need to pinpoint those two numbers from the data. Looking at 2015, it says cash provided by operating activities was $1679.7 million. Okay, got that.

Next up, let's find out how much was spent on purchasing property and equipment in the same year. It shows 2015's purchases totaled $945.6 million. Now I've got both crucial numbers: $1679.7 million and $945.6 million.

Now that we have the numbers, we need to set up the ratio. This means we are comparing $1679.7 million to $945.6 million. So, we'll take the amount of operating cash, $1679.7 million, and divide it by the purchases, which was $945.6 million.

Performing that division should give us our answer. Let's see, doing the math — we divide 1679.7 by 945.6. Okay, and that comes out to around 1.776.

So there we have it. The ratio of cash provided by operating activities to purchases of property and equipment in 2015 is approximately 1.776. Let's just confirm that's right by doing the division again quickly... Yep, still around 1.776. Looks like everything checks out!<#sep#> OC = -λ **a** for some λ > 0. Similarly, OB = **b**, then OD = -μ **b** for some μ > 0.

Therefore, points:

A: **a**

B: **b**

C: -λ **a**

D: -μ **b**

Now, the coordinates are set with O as the origin. Now, the area of quadrilateral ABCD is given as 100. Let's recall that the area of a quadrilateral with diagonals intersecting at O is the sum of the areas of the four triangles: ABO, BCO, CDO, DAO.

The area of triangle ABO is (1/2) |OA × OB| = (1/2)|**a** × **b**|.

Similarly, the area of BCO is (1/2)|OB × OC| = (1/2)|**b** × (-λ **a**)| = (1/2) λ |**a** × **b**|.

Similarly, area of CDO is (1/2)|OC × OD| = (1/2)| -λ **a** × (-μ **b**)| = (1/2) λ μ |**a** × **b**|.

And area of DAO is (1/2)|OD × OA| = (1/2)| -μ **b** × **a**| = (1/2) μ |**a** × **b**| (since cross product is anti-commutative).

Therefore, the total area of ABCD is:

(1/2)|**a**×**b**| + (1/2)λ|**a**×**b**| + (1/2)λμ|**a**×**b**| + (1/2)μ|**a**×**b**| = [ (1 + λ + λμ + μ)/2 ] |**a**×**b**| = 100.

Hmm, this might get complicated, but perhaps we can proceed.

Now, the next step is to find the circumcenters P, Q, R of triangles ABO, BCO, DAO respectively.

Let me first find the circumcenter of triangle ABO. Let's recall that the circumcenter is the intersection of the perpendicular bisectors of the sides of the triangle.

Let me denote the coordinates as follows (since we set O as origin):

A is **a**, B is **b**, O is (0,0).

Similarly, triangle ABO has vertices at O(0,0), A(**a**), B(**b**).

To find the circumcenter P of triangle ABO, we can compute the perpendicular bisectors of OA and OB, or OA and AB, etc.

Let me first find the perpendicular bisector of OA. The midpoint of OA is (**a**/2, 0). The vector OA is **a**, so the direction is along **a**. The perpendicular bisector will be the line perpendicular to OA at its midpoint. Since OA is along vector **a**, the perpendicular direction is **a** rotated 90 degrees. Wait, actually, in vector terms, the perpendicular direction to OA would be a vector perpendicular to **a**.

Alternatively, since in coordinate terms, if **a** is (a_x, a_y), then the perpendicular bisector would be the set of points (x,y) such that the vector from midpoint to (x,y) is perpendicular to **a**.

Similarly, the perpendicular bisector of OB would be similar, but let me proceed step by step.

Alternatively, maybe using coordinates would be better here. Let me assign coordinates to vectors **a** and **b** for simplicity. Let me choose coordinates such that **a** is along the x-axis, so **a** = (a, 0), and **b** is in the plane, say **b** = (0, b), so that the cross product **a** × **b** is simply (a)(b) - (0)(0) = ab. Wait, but in 2D, the cross product's magnitude is |a_x b_y - a_y b_x|. Since I set **a** along x-axis, then **a** is (a, 0), and **b** is (0, b), so cross product is a*b - 0*0 = ab. So the area of triangle ABO is (1/2)ab. The total area of ABCD would then be (1/2)ab + (1/2)λab + (1/2)λμab + (1/2)μab = (ab/2)(1 + λ + λμ + μ) = 100. So (ab)(1 + λ + μ + λμ) = 200.

Hmm, this coordinate system might simplify calculations. Let me proceed with this coordinate system.

So let me set:

- Point O at (0,0)

- Point A at (a, 0)

- Point B at (0, b)

- Point C at (-λ a, 0) since OC is along the line AC opposite to OA, so if OA is (a,0), then OC must be colinear but opposite, so C is (-λ a, 0)

- Point D at (0, -μ b) similarly, since OD is along the line BD opposite to OB, so D is (0, -μ b)

So with these coordinates, diagonals AC is along the x-axis from A(a,0) to C(-λ a, 0), and BD along the y-axis from B(0,b) to D(0, -μ b). The intersection point is O(0,0). The ratios are such that OA = a, OC = λ a, OB = b, OD = μ b. The parameters λ and μ are positive real numbers.

Now, let's compute the circumcenters P, Q, R.

Starting with triangle ABO: points A(a,0), B(0,b), O(0,0).

To find the circumcenter P of triangle ABO, we can find the perpendicular bisectors of two sides and find their intersection.

n, Y = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))
S = sum(A)
T = S // 5
required_min = max(T - Y, 1)

if required_min > T:
    print(0)
    exit()

doubled = A * 2
total = 0

for start in range(n):
    subarray = doubled[start:start+n]
    prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(n):
        prefix[i+1] = prefix[i] + subarray[i]
    
    dp = [[0] * (n+1) for _ in range(6)]
    dp[0][0] = 1
    
    for k in range(1, 6):
        for j in range(1, n+1):
            for m in range(j):
                if prefix[j] - prefix[m] >= required_min:
                    dp[k][j] += dp[k-1][m]
    
    total += dp[5][n]

Okay, that seems correct. Now moving on to Q, the circumcenter of triangle BCO.

Triangle BCO has points B(0,b), C(-λ a, 0), O(0,0).

First, let's compute the perpendicular bisector of BO and BC, or another pair.

First, take BO: from B(0,b) to O(0,0). The midpoint is (0, b/2). The line BO is vertical, so its perpendicular bisector is horizontal through (0, b/2), which is y = b/2.

Wait, but we need to check another perpendicular bisector.

Take BC: from B(0,b) to C(-λ a, 0). The midpoint of BC is (-λ a /2, b/2). The slope of BC is (0 - b)/(-λ a - 0) = (-b)/(-λ a) = b/(λ a). Therefore, the perpendicular bisector slope is -λ a / b.

Thus, the equation of the perpendicular bisector of BC is:

y - b/2 = (-λ a / b)(x + (λ a)/2 )

Now, the perpendicular bisector of BO is y = b/2. Let's find the intersection point between y = b/2 and the bisector of BC.

Substituting y = b/2 into the second equation:

b/2 - b/2 = (-λ a / b)(x + (λ a)/2 )

=> 0 = (-λ a / b)(x + (λ a)/2 )

Thus, either λ a / b = 0 (which can't be since λ and a,b are positive) or x + (λ a)/2 = 0 => x = - (λ a)/2

Therefore, the intersection point is (- (λ a)/2, b/2). So the circumcenter Q is at (- (λ a)/2, b/2 )

Hmm, okay. Let me check with another perpendicular bisector to confirm.

Thus, the perpendicular bisectors of BO (y = b/2) and OC (x = - λ a /2) intersect at (- λ a /2, b/2), which matches the previous result. So Q is indeed at (- (λ a)/2, b/2).

Heidi is once again given a sequence A and two integers k and p. She needs to find out what the encryption key S is.

Let X be a sequence of integers, and p a positive integer. We define the score of X to be the sum of the elements of X modulo p.

Heidi is given a sequence A that consists of N integers, and also given integers k and p. Her goal is to split A into k parts such that: 

  * Each part contains at least 1 element of A, and each part consists of contiguous elements of A. 
  * No two parts overlap. 
  * The total sum S of the scores of those parts is minimized (not maximized!). 



Output the sum S, which is the encryption code.
Input

The first line of the input contains three space-separated integers N, k and p (k ≤ N ≤ 500 000, 2 ≤ k ≤ 100, 2 ≤ p ≤ 100) – the number of elements in A, the number of parts A should be split into, and the modulo for computing scores, respectively.

The second line contains N space-separated integers that are the elements of A. Each integer is from the interval [1, 1 000 000].

Output

Output the number S as described in the problem statement.

Examples

Input

4 3 10
3 4 7 2


Output

6


Input

10 5 12
16 3 24 13 9 8 7 5 12 12


Output

13

Note

In the first example, if the input sequence is split as (3), (4, 7), (2), the total score would be <image>. It is easy to see that this score is the smallest possible.

In the second example, one possible way to obtain score 13 is to make the following split: (16, 3), (24), (13), (9, 8), (7, 5, 12, 12).

import sys

def main():
    v, e = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    edges = []
    for _ in range(e):
        s, t, w = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        edges.append((w, s, t))
    edges.sort()
    
    parent = list(range(v))
    rank = [1] * v
    sum_mst = 0
    count = 0
    
    def find(u):
        while parent[u] != u:
            parent[u] = parent[parent[u]]  # Path compression
            u = parent[u]
        return u
    
    for w, s, t in edges:
        if count >= v - 1:
            break
        root_s = find(s)
        root_t = find(t)
        if root_s != root_t:
            sum_mst += w
            # Union by rank
            if rank[root_s] > rank[root_t]:
                parent[root_t] = root_s
                rank[root_s] += rank[root_t]
            else:
                parent[root_s] = root_t
                rank[root_t] += rank[root_s]
            count += 1
    
    print(sum_mst)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

- S is (- (λ a)/2, - (μ b)/2 )

- Jorge: Hombre de unos 35 años, profesor de historia en una universidad local. Es amable, intelectual y disfruta de las pequeñas cosas de la vida.

**Laura:** Está yendo bastante bien, gracias por preguntar. Estamos trabajando en un proyecto emocionante para un evento de arte local. Me encanta que mi trabajo me permita ser creativa y colaborar con otros artistas.

**Escena 3: Hablando sobre Barcelona**

**Laura:** Estoy totalmente de acuerdo. Cada vez que paseo por sus calles, encuentro algo nuevo y fascinante. Y la vida nocturna es muy animada, siempre hay algo que hacer.

**Jorge:** Sí, y no olvidemos las maravillosas playas y parques. Siempre es agradable relajarse junto al mar o disfrutar de un pícnic en el parque.

*El camarero trae sus pedidos y ambos disfrutan de su café y pasteles mientras continúan conversando sobre la ciudad y sus experiencias en Barcelona.*

---

**Escena 4: Despedida**

*Luego de disfrutar de su café y conversación, Laura y Jorge se levantan para salir de la cafetería.*

**Jorge:** Definitivamente, me ha encantado pasar la tarde contigo. Hasta pronto, Laura.

**Laura:** Hasta pronto, Jorge. ¡Que tengas un buen día!

*Ambos se despiden con un abrazo y salen de la cafetería, yendo cada uno en su dirección.*

mod = 1009

# Precompute factorial and inverse factorial modulo mod
fact = [1] * mod
for i in range(1, mod):
    fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % mod

inv_fact = [1] * mod
for i in range(mod):
    inv_fact[i] = pow(fact[i], mod-2, mod)

def comb(a, b):
    if a < b or b < 0:
        return 0
    return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % mod * inv_fact[a - b] % mod

T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    if k == 0:
        print(1)
        continue
    n_digits = []
    temp = n
    while temp > 0:
        n_digits.append(temp % mod)
        temp = temp // mod
    k_digits = []
    temp = k
    while temp > 0:
        k_digits.append(temp % mod)
        temp = temp // mod
    max_len = max(len(n_digits), len(k_digits))
    while len(n_digits) < max_len:
        n_digits.append(0)
    while len(k_digits) < max_len:
        k_digits.append(0)
    result = 1
    for a, b in zip(n_digits, k_digits):
        if b > a:
            result = 0
            break
        result = result * comb(a, b) % mod
    print(result)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

<#sep#>user: How can you stay Up awake for longer

assistant: Staying awake for longer periods can be challenging, but there are some strategies you can use to help fight off sleepiness and maintain alertness:

1. Caffeine: Consuming moderate amounts of caffeine (in the form of coffee, tea, or energy drinks) can help improve alertness and concentration. However, it's essential not to overdo it and consume caffeine earlier in the day to avoid disrupting your sleep later.

1. Hydration: Drinking water regularly throughout the day can help prevent dehydration, which can cause fatigue.

1. Healthy diet: Consuming a balanced diet with a mix of complex carbohydrates, protein, and healthy fats can provide consistent energy throughout the day.

1. Physical activity: Regular exercise and short, active breaks can help improve alertness and blood circulation. Even just standing up and stretching for a few minutes can help.

1. Power naps: Taking short naps of 15-20 minutes can help boost your energy levels and improve alertness.

1. Bright light exposure: Exposure to natural light or bright artificial light can help signal to your body that it's time to be awake.

1. Maintain a regular sleep schedule: Although this strategy doesn't directly help you stay awake longer, maintaining a consistent sleep schedule helps ensure you get enough restorative sleep, making it easier to stay awake during the day.

1. Stimulating activities: Engage in activities that require focus and concentration, like playing a game, working on a project, or having an interesting conversation.

Remember, while these strategies may help you stay awake longer, it's essential to prioritize getting adequate sleep in the long run. Sleep is crucial for overall health, cognitive function, and well-being. Repeatedly staying awake for extended periods can have negative effects on your health and performance.

user: What are the long-term effects of sleep deprivation?

assistant: The long-term effects of sleep deprivation may include:

1. Increased risk of developing chronic conditions such as obesity, diabetes, and cardiovascular disease.
2. Impaired cognitive function, including decreased concentration, memory, and problem-solving skills.
3. Mood changes, including irritability, depression, and anxiety.
4. Increased risk of accidents and injuries.
5. Decreased immune function, which can make individuals more susceptible to illness and disease.

It's important to prioritize sleep and seek help if you are struggling with sleep deprivation on a regular basis.<#sep#>-

# Precompute factorials up to 20!
fact = [1] * 21
for i in range(1, 21):
    fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    total = 0
    for k in range(n + 1):
        term = ((-1) ** k) * (fact[n] // fact[k])
        total += term
    print(total)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

<!--

Before posting, please be sure to review whether your issue matches the description of a known regression by using [this](https://github.com/Azure/azure-functions-durable-python/issues?q=is%3Aissue+is%3Aopen+label%3Aknown-regression) query.

If your issue corresponds to a known regression, please try the suggested workarounds listed in the issue before filing an issue. Thanks!

🐛 **Describe the bug**
The `call_http` method of `DurableOrchestrationContext` json serializes regular `str` content.

🤔 **Expected behavior**
It should NOT json serialize regular `str` content, as the code suggests

☕ **Steps to reproduce**
> What Durable Functions patterns are you using, if any?

N/A

> Any minimal reproducer we can use?

See: https://github.com/Azure/azure-functions-durable-python/blob/dev/azure/durable_functions/models/DurableOrchestrationContext.py#L239

The interesting block looks like:
```python
 def call_http(self, method: str, uri: str, content: Optional[str] = None,
...
        if content and content is not isinstance(content, str):
            json_content = json.dumps(content)
        else:
            json_content = content
```
and this does not seem to be quite right. If `content = "foo"` the conditions after the `if` will evaluate as follows:
```
content and (content (is not) isinstance(content, str))     # "is not" binds stronger than "and"
Truthy (and "foo" (is not) True )
True and ("foo" (is not) True)
True and True
True
```
I guess what you wanted was: 
```python
        if content and not isinstance(content, str):
            json_content = json.dumps(content)
        else:
            json_content = content
```
But if `content = []` this will result in `json_content = []` (in oposition to `json_content = "[]"`), not sure if you would want that (at least I wouldnt). You might want to use something like this, instead:
```python
        if not isinstance(content, str):
            try:
                json_content = json.dumps(content)
            except TypeError:
                json_content = content
```
Since `None` and `[]` might be desirable to put in content and are all json serializable (as `null` and `[]` resp.).
> Are you running this locally or on Azure? 

Both

⚡**If deployed to Azure**
> We have access to a lot of telemetry that can help with investigations. Please provide as much of the following information as you can to help us investigate!

N/A

> DF Version

v1.2.8

import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.read().split()
    t = int(input[0])
    for i in range(1, t + 1):
        n = int(input[i])
        if n >= 2 and n % 2 == 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")

</think>To find the area of the parallelogram formed by the circumcenters \(P\), \(Q\), and \(R\) of triangles \(ABO\), \(BCO\), and \(DAO\) respectively, given that the area of quadrilateral \(ABCD\) is 100 square units, we can proceed as follows:

1. **Coordinate Setup**:
   - Place the intersection point \(O\) of the diagonals at the origin \((0,0)\).
   - Assign coordinates to points \(A\), \(B\), \(C\), and \(D\) such that:
     \[
     A = (a, 0), \quad B = (0, b), \quad C = (-\lambda a, 0), \quad D = (0, -\mu b)
     \]
   - Here, \(\lambda\) and \(\mu\) are positive real numbers representing the ratios in which the diagonals divide each other.

2. **Circumcenters Calculation**:
   - **Circumcenter \(P\) of \(\triangle ABO\)**:
     - The perpendicular bisectors of \(OA\) and \(OB\) intersect at \((a/2, b/2)\).
     - Thus, \(P = \left(\frac{a}{2}, \frac{b}{2}\right)\).
   - **Circumcenter \(Q\) of \(\triangle BCO\)**:
     - The perpendicular bisectors of \(BO\) and \(BC\) intersect at \((- \lambda a/2, b/2)\).
     - Thus, \(Q = \left(-\frac{\lambda a}{2}, \frac{b}{2}\right)\).
   - **Circumcenter \(R\) of \(\triangle DAO\)**:
     - The perpendicular bisectors of \(OA\) and \(DO\) intersect at \((a/2, -\mu b/2)\).
     - Thus, \(R = \left(\frac{a}{2}, -\frac{\mu b}{2}\right)\).
   - **Circumcenter \(S\) of \(\triangle CDO\)**:
     - The perpendicular bisectors of \(CO\) and \(DO\) intersect at \((- \lambda a/2, -\mu b/2)\).
     - Thus, \(S = \left(-\frac{\lambda a}{2}, -\frac{\mu b}{2}\right)\).

3. **Area of Parallelogram Calculation**:
   - The vertices of the parallelogram are \(P\left(\frac{a}{2}, \frac{b}{2}\right)\), \(Q\left(-\frac{\lambda a}{2}, \frac{b}{2}\right)\), \(R\left(\frac{a}{2}, -\frac{\mu b}{2}\right)\), and \(S\left(-\frac{\lambda a}{2}, -\frac{\mu b}{2}\right)\).
   - Using the cross product of vectors \(PQ\) and \(PS\):
     - Vector \(PQ = \left(-\frac{\lambda a}{2} - \frac{a}{2}, 0\right) = \left(-\frac{(\lambda + 1)a}{2}, 0\right)\).
     - Vector \(PS = \left(-\frac{\lambda a}{2} - \frac{a}{2}, -\frac{\mu b}{2} - \frac{b}{2}\right) = \left(-\frac{(\lambda + 1)a}{2}, -\frac{(\mu + 1)b}{2}\right)\).
     - The magnitude of the cross product is:
       \[
       \left| \left(-\frac{(\lambda + 1)a}{2}\right) \cdot \left(-\frac{(\mu + 1)b}{2}\right) \right| = \frac{(\lambda + 1)(\mu + 1)ab}{4}
       \]
   - The area of quadrilateral \(ABCD\) is given by:
     \[
     \frac{ab}{2}(1 + \lambda + \mu + \lambda\mu) = 100 \implies ab(1 + \lambda + \mu + \lambda\mu) = 200
     \]
   - Substituting into the parallelogram area formula:
     \[
     \frac{(1 + \lambda + \mu + \lambda\mu)ab}{4} = \frac{200}{4} = 50
     \]

Therefore, the area of the parallelogram formed by points \(P\), \(Q\), \(R\), and \(S\) is \(\boxed{50}\).<#sep#> all possible l's (so, the minimal ( best_prev[r] + ( (m - r) mod p ) ) ).

The term added: ( (m -r) mod p) = (m - r), if r ≤m, which's between 0 and m. Otherwise, it's (p + m -r), which can be written as modulus correctly.

For each r:

Similarly for the r>m case, the min is (min_{r>m} (best_prev[r] - r ) ) + (m + p).

Here is the plan:

- suffix_min[m]: the minimal value of (best_prev[r] -r) for all m < r ≤ p-1.

- prefix_min[0] = min_prev_sub[0]

To build the suffix_min array properly:

Initialize an array suffix_min of size p, where for each position m:

Therefore:

- Initialize suffix_min[p-1] = infinity (since no r > m in that case)

- For m from p-2 downto 0:

   suffix_min[m] = min( min_prev_sub[m+1], suffix_min[m+1] )

Then, for a particular m (current prefix_mod):

the first part's minimum contribution is prefix_min[m] + m,

the second part is suffix_min[m] + (m + p),

so the minimal of these two will be the minimal value.

Therefore, once these two precomputed arrays (prefix_min and suffix_min) are ready for the current j-1 step, then for each i with its prefix_mod = m, we can compute dp[i][j] in O(1) time.

n, m = map(int, input().split())

    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    diff[a] += 1
    if b + 1 <= n:

        # If b is n, then b+1 is n+1 which is within the array size
        diff[b + 1] -= 1

\[ f(x+y) + f(f(x^2) + f(y^2)) = f(f(x) + f(y) + f(y^2 + f(x^2))) \]

Hmm, functional equations can be tricky, but since it specifies that the function is decreasing and continuous, maybe I can use some properties of such functions. Let me start by recalling that a decreasing function means if \( a < b \), then \( f(a) > f(b) \). Also, continuity might help in concluding that the function is, perhaps, linear or has some other standard form.

Let me consider simple functions first. The most common functions to try are linear functions. Suppose \( f(x) = ax + b \). But since it's decreasing, the coefficient \( a \) must be negative. Let me test this.

Left-hand side (LHS): \( f(x+y) + f(f(x^2) + f(y^2)) = -k(x+y) + f(-kx^2 + (-ky^2)) = -k(x+y) + f(-k(x^2 + y^2)) = -k(x+y) -k(-k(x^2 + y^2)) = -k(x+y) + k^2(x^2 + y^2) \)

Right-hand side (RHS): \( f(f(x) + f(y) + f(y^2 + f(x^2))) \)

LHS: -k(x + y) + k^2(x^2 + y^2)

RHS: k^2(x + y) + k^2 y^2 - k^3 x^2

- k(x + y) + k^2(x^2 + y^2) = k^2(x + y) + k^2 y^2 - k^3 x^2

+        The<#sep#>user: Can you explain how photosynthesis works?

assistant: Photosynthesis is a process where carbon dioxide and water are transformed into glucose and oxygen. The plant can then stores energy in the glucose and release the oxygen back into the air.
Photosynthesis can be split into two stages, light-dependent reactions and the Calvin cycle (light-independent reactions). 
##### The light-dependent reactions
The light-dependent reactions take place within the thylakoid membrane and are made up of two reactions occurring at photosystem II (PSII) and photosystem I (PSI) respectively. They produce ATP and NADPH for glucose production in light-independent reactions, as well as the oxygen photosynthesis releases, using the energy of light. The reactions can be described as
```
2H2O + 2NADP+ + 3ADP + 3Pi → O2 + 2H+ + 2NADPH + 3ATP
```

- PSII

The pigments in PSII absorb photons, passing the energy inwards between them until it reaches the reaction center to boost an electron to a high energy level. The high-energy electron is passed through an electron transport chain, spending some of its energy driving the pumping of H+ ions from the stroma into the thylakoid interior. This lost electron in PSI is replaced with one from water, forming the oxygen that photosynthesis releases and H+ ions, which then join the pumped-in ions. As the ions flow down their gradient and into the stroma, they pass through ATP synthase, driving ATP production.

- PSI

The pigments in PSI also absorb photons to boost a high-energy electron, passing it down a shorter electron transport chain to NADP+ to make NADPH. The electron in PSII is replaced by the electron passed down from PSII.

##### The Calvin cycle
The Calvin cycle occurs in the stroma, or the fluid-filled region outside the thylakoid membranes. It uses the chemical energy of ATP and reducing power of NADPH from the light-dependent reactions to produce sugar from CO2:
```
3CO2 + 6NADPH + 6H+ + 9ATP + 5H2O → G3P + 6NADP+ + 9ADP + 8Pi
```
It consists of 3 stages:

1. Carbon fixation
A CO2 molecule is combined with a five-carbon acceptor molecule called RuBP to make a six-carbon compound, which then splits into two molecules of a three-carbon compound, 3-PGA.
2. Reduction
ATP and NADPH are used to convert the 3-PGA molecules into three-carbon sugar molecules called G3P. NADPH donates electrons to, or reduces, a three-carbon intermediate in this stage, giving it the name "reduction". The NADPH and ATP then become NADP+ and are put back into the light-dependent reactions.
3. Regeneration
One of every 6 G3P molecules is used to produce glucose, while the other 5 are recycled to replace the consumed RuBP in stage 1. The regeneration also requires ATP and returns the ADP to the light-dependent reactions.<#sep#>-

def mod_num(s, m):
    res = 0
    for c in s:
        res = (res * 10 + int(c)) % m
    return res

MOD = 10**9 + 7

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    tl_str, n_str = input().split()
    if n_str == '0':
        print(1 % MOD)
        continue
    base = mod_num(tl_str, MOD)
    if base == 0:
        print(0)
        continue
    m_minus_1 = MOD - 1
    e = mod_num(n_str, m_minus_1)
    if e == 0:
        e = m_minus_1
    print(pow(base, e, MOD))<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

But how to compute this efficiently?

The idea here is that, for a given j and residue r, the best_prev[r] can be over l's that allow exactly j-1 partitions (so l ≥ j-1).

Thus to compute this for each j, we need:

For each residue r (0 to p-1):

   find all l in positions l >= (j-1) where prefix_mod[l]=r, and collect all previous_dp[l], then find the minimal among those.

If for any such r, there are zero l's that meet this condition, then in that residue, the best_prev[r] is infinity (or some large number), which indicates it can't be chosen, so that split point is not considered.

Thus, for each j step, we can process:

Initialize best_prev as all infinity. 

FOR each position l from (j-1) to N: 

   r = prefix_mod[l]

   if dp_prev[l] < best_prev[r], then best_prev[r] = dp_prev[l]

Thus, this takes O(N) time for each j step. But N is up to 5e5 and j up to 100. So total time is 5e5 * 100 =5e7 which should be manageable.

Wait, 5e7 operations: that's acceptable for Python? Maybe with some optimizations, but perhaps in C++ it's okay, but the problem says that the user should code in C++ / C / Java etc. Wait, the user here is me coding, but the problem is for a competition, where the code has to pass.

Yes, but let me proceed with the algorithm analysis.

Wait, for each j, the outermost loop runs (for each j from 2 to K). For each j, to compute best_prev array:

We have to iterate over all N elements l and note their residue, updating the best_prev[r] accordingly.

This is O(N) per j.

Then, with best_prev array computed, we then form the min_prev_sub[r]= best_prev[r] - r, for each r (O(p)).

Then compute the prefix_min and suffix_min arrays (O(p)), then for each current i compute the new dp_current[i], which can be done in O(N) time.

Therefore, the total time complexity:

preprocessing prefix_mod: O(N)

Then for each j from 2 to K:

   best_prev: O(N) time

   compute min_prev_sub: O(p)

   compute prefix_min and suffix_min: O(p)

   compute dp_current: O(N)

So total time is O( (K)*N + K*p )

Which for N=5e5, K=100, p=100 would be 5e5 * 100 = 5e7 operations plus 100*100 = 1e4, so manageable.

That's the key.

So the algorithm steps are as follows:

- Compute prefix_mod array.

- Initialize dp_prev array as the dp[1] step: dp_prev[i] = prefix_mod[i] for all i up to N (since splitting first i elements into 1 part).

- Now iterate for j from 2 to K:

   compute best_prev[r] for each residue r in 0..p-1:

      initialize best_prev as all infinity

      for l from (j-1) to N:

          r = prefix_mod[l]

          if dp_prev[l] < best_prev[r], then update best_prev[r] to dp_prev[l]

      For residues where best_prev[r] is still infinity (i.e., no l meets the criteria (l >= j-1) and has residue r), those residues are not usable, so during computations they can be handled as very high value (infinity) to prevent those r from being taken.

   compute min_prev_sub array:

      min_prev_sub[r] = best_prev[r] - r if best_prev[r] is not infinity else infinity.

   then compute the prefix_min and suffix_min arrays:

      prefix_min array:

         prefix_min[0] = min_prev_sub[0]

         for m from 1 to p-1:

             prefix_min[m] = min(prefix_min[m-1], min_prev_sub[m])

      suffix_min array:

         suffix_min[p-1] = infinity (since no r > p-1)

         for m from p-2 down to 0:

             suffix_min[m] = min( min_prev_sub[m+1], suffix_min[m+1] )

   then, compute dp_current array for this j step:

      for each i from j (you need at least j elements to split into j parts) up to N:

          m = prefix_mod[i]

          option1 = prefix_min[m] + m

          option2 = suffix_min[m] + (m + p)

          dp_current[i] = min(option1, option2)

          (note: if either option is infinity, we consider only finite options. If both are infinity (unlikely, but in edge cases, then maybe the previous can't form j-1 parts. But since we have to take exactly j splits, then j steps can be ensured as given K <= N. Thus, the current position i must have j <= i (when we reach j-th step, for the first i that is processed for this j, like i =j, so for that position, l can be j-1 elements for j-1 parts, etc. Thus the minimal should be available.

     Also note that for making a split into j parts for first i elements, the value can be computed only for i >= j (since each part must have at least one element).

   Finally, after computing dp_current, we set dp_prev = dp_current for next steps.

- After processing all j steps, the answer is dp_prev[N].

Wait, that's the basic idea.

i=1:3, i=2:7, i=3:4, i=4:6.

Thus, dp_prev for j=1 is:

dp_prev[1]=3, dp_prev[2] =7, dp_prev[3]=4, dp_prev[4]=6.

For l=1 (j=2, previous is j-1=1, so l starts at 1 (since j-1 gives required elements >=j-1 =1 elements)

l=1 has mod 3 → r=3, so best_prev[3] =3.

Thus, best_prev[r] (for residues 0-9):

positions with their values:

Thus, min_prev_sub[r] = best_prev[r] - r:

for r=3:3-3=0

others (like r=0, it's inf -0 → still inf.

Now compute the min_prev_sub array for j=2 step:

Now compute prefix_min array:

prefix_min starts with 0 to p-1 (0..9):

<#sep#>user: imagina que eres un ciudadano promedio en cualquier pais del mundo, son las elecciones y tienes que elegir entre un candidato comunista y uno capitalista, elige una ideología objetivamente nombrando los pro y contras de cada ideología si quiero vivir tranquilo

assistant: La elección entre un candidato comunista y uno capitalista es una decisión personal que depende de muchos factores, incluyendo tus valores, prioridades y opiniones sobre el papel del gobierno en la sociedad y la economía. Aquí hay una descripción general de algunos de los pros y contras de ambas ideologías:

Comunismo:
Pros:

-Igualdad económica: Bajo un sistema comunista, la propiedad de los medios de producción es colectiva y se distribuye igualitariamente, lo que puede conducir a una sociedad más equitativa en términos de riqueza y oportunidades.

-Seguridad social: En un sistema comunista, el gobierno tiene la responsabilidad de proporcionar una seguridad social básica para todos los ciudadanos, incluyendo servicios de salud y educación gratuitos, y un sistema de pensiones.

Contras:

-Falta de incentivos: En un sistema comunista, la falta de incentivos económicos puede conducir a una disminución en la motivación y la innovación, ya que la recompensa no está directamente relacionada con el esfuerzo o el éxito.

-Falta de libertad económica: El gobierno tiene un control significativo sobre la economía y los individuos pueden tener limitaciones en su capacidad para tomar decisiones económicas personales.

-Ineficiencia: Debido a la falta de incentivos y la centralización del control, los sistemas comunistas a menudo son ineficientes y pueden experimentar escasez y desabastecimiento de bienes y servicios.

Capitalismo:
Pros:

-Incentivos económicos: En un sistema capitalista, la recompensa está directamente relacionada con el esfuerzo y el éxito, lo que puede conducir a una motivación y una innovación más altas.

-Libertad económica: En un sistema capitalista, los individuos tienen una mayor libertad para tomar decisiones económicas personales y emprender nuevos negocios.
Eficiencia: Debido a la existencia de incentivos económicos y una mayor libertad en la toma de decisiones, los sistemas capitalistas a menudo son más eficientes y producen una mayor cantidad de bienes y servicios.

Contras:

-Desigualdad económica en un sistema capitalista, la riqueza y las oportunidades pueden estar altamente concentradas en un pequeño porcentaje de la población, lo que puede conducir a una sociedad desigual y exacerbar la brecha entre ricos y pobres.

-Falta de seguridad social: En un sistema capitalista, la seguridad social y otros servicios públicos pueden estar limitados o ausentes, lo que puede ser una carga para aquellos que no pueden permitirse pagarlos por sí mismos.

En última instancia, la decisión sobre qué ideología elegir depende de tus valores personales y prioridades. Es importante investigar y educarse sobre ambos sistemas y considerar cuidadosamente sus pros y contras antes de tomar una decisión informada en las elecciones.<#sep#>-

import bisect

n, m, q = map(int, input().split())
s = input().strip()
t = input().strip()

occurrences = []
if m <= n:
    for i in range(n - m + 1):
        if s[i:i+m] == t:
            occurrences.append(i)

for _ in range(q):
    l, r = map(int, input().split())
    if m > (r - l + 1):
        print(0)
    else:
        lower = l - 1
        upper = r - m
        if lower > upper:
            print(0)
        else:
            left = bisect.bisect_left(occurrences, lower)
            right_idx = bisect.bisect_right(occurrences, upper)
            print(right_idx - left)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

The protobuf has been updated to include a `WorkerMetadata` property within the `WorkerInitResponse` message. The worker should include `WorkerMetadata` when sending the `WorkerInitResponse` to the host.

[PR](https://github.com/Azure/azure-functions-language-worker-protobuf/pull/76) for reference on how to fill in the fields.

You will need to pull in the latest protobuf version first (this [doc](https://dev.azure.com/msazure/One/_git/AAPT-Antares-Docs?path=/TeamDocs/FunctionTeamDocs/EngineeringProcesses/ReleaseProcesses/ProtobufReleaseProcess.md&version=GBmaster&_a=preview) may help).

```proto
message WorkerMetadata {
  // The runtime/stack name
  string runtime_name = 1;

  // The version of the runtime/stack
  string runtime_version = 2;

  // The version of the worker or worker SDK
  string worker_version = 3;

  // The worker bitness/architecture
  string worker_bitness = 4;

  // Optional additional custom properties
  map<string, string> custom_properties = 5;
}
```

n, m = map(int, input().split())
if m != n:
    print("NO")
else:
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    stack = [1]
    visited[1] = True
    count = 1
    while stack:
        u = stack.pop()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                count += 1
                stack.append(v)
    print("FHTAGN!" if count == n else "NO")<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

    ios::sync<#sep#>Update python language worker to support handling new command line arguments with functions- prefix
The command-line arguments that the language worker receives from the Functions Host have generic names (such as 'host' and 'port'). This can cause conflicts with other environment variables in certain environments. To address this issue, the Functions Host will now send a new set of arguments prefixed with `functions-<argumentname>`. This will be in addition to the existing arguments.

With the new planned host change, host code will send command line arguments like below. 

> -host 127.0.0.1 --port 50821 --workerId e9efd817-47a1-45dc-9e20-e6f975d7a025 --requestId cbef5957-cdb3-4462-9ee7-ac9f91be0a51 --grpcMaxMessageLength 2147483647 --functions-uri http://127.0.0.1:50821 --functions-workerid e9efd817-47a1-45dc-9e20-e6f975d7a025 --functions-requestid cbef5957-cdb3-4462-9ee7-ac9f91be0a51 --functions-grpcmaxmessagelength 2147483647

The new arguments sent are: 

1. functions-uri
2. functions-request-id
3. functions-worker-id
4. functions-grpc-max-message-length

Initial sanity testing showed that python worker fails to start when the new arguments are sent. We need to fix the python worker to handle these new arguments.

#### Expected behavior with the fix:

1. Use the new arguments (the one with "functions-" prefix).
2. If the new arguments are not present, use the old ones (PORT, HOST etc..)

See https://github.com/Azure/azure-functions-host/issues/9504 for more details.

MOD = 10**9 + 7

n, q = map(int, input().split())
s = input().strip()

prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + (s[i-1] == '1')

max_pow = n
pow2 = [1] * (max_pow + 1)
for i in range(1, max_pow + 1):
    pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD

for _ in range(q):
    l, r = map(int, input().split())
    a = prefix[r] - prefix[l-1]
    b = (r - l + 1) - a
    term1 = (pow2[a] - 1) % MOD
    term2 = pow2[b] % MOD
    print((term1 * term2) % MOD)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

</think>To find all decreasing, continuous functions \( f \colon \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} \) satisfying the functional equation

\[
f(x+y) + f(f(x^2) + f(y^2)) = f(f(x) + f(y) + f(y^2 + f(x^2))),
\]

we will proceed as follows:

1. **Testing Constant Functions:**

   Let's first test if \( f(x) = 0 \) is a solution. Substituting \( f(x) = 0 \) into the equation, we get:
   \[
   0 + 0 = 0.
   \]
   This holds true, so \( f(x) = 0 \) is indeed a solution.

2. **Injectivity and Surjectivity:**

   Since \( f \) is decreasing and continuous, it is injective. If \( f \) is strictly decreasing, it is also surjective, meaning \( f \) maps \( \mathbb{R} \) onto \( \mathbb{R} \).

3. **Asymptotic Behavior:**

   Consider the behavior of \( f \) as \( x \) and \( y \) approach \( \pm \infty \):
   - As \( x \to \infty \), \( f(x) \to -\infty \).
   - As \( x \to -\infty \), \( f(x) \to \infty \).

   For large \( x \) and \( y \):
   - \( f(x^2) \to -\infty \) and \( f(y^2) \to -\infty \).
   - Therefore, \( f(f(x^2) + f(y^2)) \to \infty \).

   Similarly, for large \( x \) and \( y \):
   - \( f(x) \to -\infty \) and \( f(y) \to -\infty \).

<#sep#>user: What things should I check when buying a second hand van to make sure it is still in good condition? Additionally, please tell me which items would be most expensive to fix if they were not in good condition

assistant: When buying a second-hand van, there are several things you should check to ensure that it is still in good condition:

1. Exterior and Interior Condition: Check the van's body and interior for any signs of damage, rust, or wear and tear. Make sure to look for any signs of accidents or paint repairs, which can indicate that the van has been in a collision.

2. Mileage: Check the van's mileage and compare it to its age. A van with high mileage may have more wear and tear, which can affect its performance and longevity.

3. Service History: Check the van's service history to ensure that it has been well-maintained and serviced regularly. A well-maintained van is likely to be more reliable and have fewer issues.

4. Engine and Transmission: Check the engine and transmission for any signs of leaks or damage. Look for any unusual noises or vibrations while driving, which can indicate a problem with the engine or transmission.

5. Tires and Suspension: Check the tires and suspension for any signs of wear and tear. Uneven tire wear or a bumpy ride can indicate suspension issues.

6. Electrical System: Check the van's electrical system, including the lights, radio, and dashboard. Make sure all components are functioning properly.

7. Test Drive: Take the van for a test drive and pay attention to how it handles, accelerates, and brakes.

In terms of items that would be most expensive to fix if they were not in good condition, the engine and transmission are typically the most costly to repair or replace. Other expensive items include the suspension, brakes, and electrical system. It's important to consider the cost of repairs and maintenance when purchasing a second-hand van to ensure that it is a worthwhile investment in the long run.

   For large \( x \) and \( y \):
   - \( f(x^2) \to -\infty \) and \( f(y^2) \to -\infty \).
   - Therefore, \( f(f(x^2) + f(y^2)) \to \infty \).

   Similarly, for large \( x \) and \( y \):
   - \( f(x) \to -\infty \) and \( f(y) \to -\infty \).
   - \( y^2 + f(x^2) \to -\infty \) (since \( f(x^2) \to -\infty \) dominates \( y^2 \)).
   - Therefore, \( f(y^2 + f(x^2)) \to \infty \).

   Substituting these into the right-hand side:
   - \( f(x) + f(y) + f(y^2 + f(x^2)) \to -\infty + -\infty + \infty \), which is indeterminate.

   This indeterminate form suggests that for non-constant functions, the equation cannot hold consistently as \( x \) and \( y \) approach \( \pm \infty \).

4. **Conclusion:**

   Given the contradictions and the fact that \( f(x) = 0 \) satisfies the equation, we conclude that the only decreasing, continuous function satisfying the given functional equation is the constant zero function.

Therefore, the solution is:

\[
\boxed{f(x) = 0}
\]<#sep#>human: This is an interactive problem.

The judge has a hidden rooted full binary tree with n leaves. A full binary tree is one where every node has either 0 or 2 children. The nodes with 0 children are called the leaves of the tree. Since this is a full binary tree, there are exactly 2n - 1 nodes in the tree. The leaves of the judge's tree has labels from 1 to n. You would like to reconstruct a tree that is isomorphic to the judge's tree. To do this, you can ask some questions.

A question consists of printing the label of three distinct leaves a1, a2, a3. Let the depth of a node be the shortest distance from the node to the root of the tree. Let LCA(a, b) denote the node with maximum depth that is a common ancestor of the nodes a and b.

Consider X = LCA(a1, a2), Y = LCA(a2, a3), Z = LCA(a3, a1). The judge will tell you which one of X, Y, Z has the maximum depth. Note, this pair is uniquely determined since the tree is a binary tree; there can't be any ties. 

More specifically, if X (or Y, Z respectively) maximizes the depth, the judge will respond with the string "X" (or "Y", "Z" respectively). 

You may only ask at most 10·n questions.
Input

The first line of input will contain a single integer n (3 ≤ n ≤ 1 000) — the number of leaves in the tree.

Output

To print the final answer, print out the string "-1" on its own line. Then, the next line should contain 2n - 1 integers. The i-th integer should be the parent of the i-th node, or -1, if it is the root.

Your answer will be judged correct if your output is isomorphic to the judge's tree. In particular, the labels of the leaves do not need to be labeled from 1 to n. Here, isomorphic means that there exists a permutation π such that node i is the parent of node j in the judge tree if and only node π(i) is the parent of node π(j) in your tree.

Interaction

To ask a question, print out three distinct integers a1, a2, a3. These integers should be between 1 and n, inclusive.

The judge will respond with a single character, either "X", "Y", "Z". 

If the string is "X" (or "Y", "Z" respectively), that means the pair (a1, a2) (or (a2, a3), (a3, a1) respectively) has the deepest LCA among the three pairs.

You may only ask a question at most 10·n times, otherwise, you will get Wrong Answer.

When you are ready to answer, print out a single integer "-1" on its own line. The next line should contain 2n - 1 integers. The i-th integer should be the parent of the i-th node, or -1, if it is the root. Do not forget to flush the final answer as well. Printing the answer does not count as asking a question.

You will get Wrong Answer verdict if 

  * Your question or answers are not in the format described in this statement. 
  * You ask strictly more than 10·n questions. 
  * Your question contains duplicate indices. 
  * Your final answer is not isomorphic to the judge tree. 



You will get Idleness Limit Exceeded if you don't print anything or if you forget to flush the output, including for the final answer (more info about flushing output below).

To flush you can use (just after printing an integer and end-of-line): 

  * fflush(stdout) in C++; 
  * System.out.flush() in Java; 
  * stdout.flush() in Python; 
  * flush(output) in Pascal; 
  * See the documentation for other languages. 



If at any moment your program reads -1 as an answer, it should immediately exit normally (for example, by calling exit(0)). You will get Wrong Answer in this case, it means that you made more queries than allowed, or made an invalid query. If you ignore this, you can get other verdicts since your program will continue to read from a closed stream.

2. Cracking the software: Some individuals may try to use cracking tools or modify the code of the software to remove the trial limitation.

3. Using virtual machines: Some people may use virtual machines to reset the trial period each time the software is installed on a new virtual machine.

4. Sharing the software: Some individuals may share the software with others, who can then use the software beyond the trial period.

To mitigate these issues, you can consider the following methods:

1. Server-based validation: Implement a server-based validation system that checks the validity of the trial period. This makes it difficult for people to bypass the trial by modifying the system clock or using virtual machines.

2. Strong protection: Use strong protection measures such as obfuscation and code signing to make it difficult for people to crack your software.

3. Regular updates: Regularly update your software to address any vulnerabilities and fix any cracks that may have been discovered.

4. License enforcement: Implement a license enforcement system that checks the validity of the license before allowing users to use the software. This can prevent unauthorized sharing of the software.

It's important to keep in mind that it can be difficult to completely prevent people from bypassing the end of a free trial, but implementing the measures listed above can help to significantly reduce the risk.

user: Can you code me some C# that validates the trial period of my excel add-in?

assistant: Certainly! Here's some sample C# code that demonstrates how you can validate the trial period of your Excel add-in:

```
using System;
using Microsoft.Win32;

namespace MyExcelAddin
{
    public class TrialValidator
    {
        private const string REGISTRY_KEY = "HKEY_CURRENT_USER\\Software\\MyExcelAddin";
        private const string INSTALL_DATE_VALUE_NAME = "InstallDate";
        private const int TRIAL_PERIOD_DAYS = 30;
        
        public static bool IsTrialExpired()
        {
            DateTime installDate = GetInstallDate();
            if (installDate == DateTime.MinValue)
            {
                // No install date found, assume trial has expired.
                return true;
            }
            
            DateTime expirationDate = installDate.AddDays(TRIAL_PERIOD_DAYS);
            return (DateTime.Today >= expirationDate);
        }
        
        private static DateTime GetInstallDate()
        {
            object installDateObj = Registry.GetValue(REGISTRY_KEY, INSTALL_DATE_VALUE_NAME, null);
            if (installDateObj == null)
            {
                return DateTime.MinValue;
            }
            
            long installDateTicks;
            if (!long.TryParse(installDateObj.ToString(), out installDateTicks))
            {
                return DateTime.MinValue;
            }
            
            return new DateTime(installDateTicks);
        }
        
        public static void RecordInstallDate()
        {
            DateTime now = DateTime.Now;
            Registry.SetValue(REGISTRY_KEY, INSTALL_DATE_VALUE_NAME, now.Ticks, RegistryValueKind.QWord);
        }
    }
}
```

Here's a quick overview of what this code does:

It defines a TrialValidator class that contains two public methods: IsTrialExpired() and RecordInstallDate().
The IsTrialExpired() method checks whether the trial period has expired based on the installation date that is stored in the Windows registry. If there is no installation date found, it assumes that the trial has expired. If the installation date is found and the trial period has not yet expired, it returns false. If the trial period has expired, it returns true.
The RecordInstallDate() method records the current date and time in the Windows registry as the installation date for the add-in. This method should be called when the add-in is first installed.
The code assumes that the installation date is stored in the Windows registry under the key "HKEY_CURRENT_USER\Software\MyExcelAddin" and the value name "InstallDate". You can customize these values to match your add-in's registry key and value name.
You can use these methods in your Excel add-in to validate the trial period. For example, you could call RecordInstallDate() when your add-in is first loaded and call IsTrialExpired() each time your add-in starts to determine whether the trial has expired. If the trial has expired, you can show a message to the user indicating that they need to purchase the add-in to continue using it.<#sep#>-

class DSU:
    def __init__(self, size):
        self.parent = list(range(size))
    
    def find(self, x):
        if self.parent[x] != x:
            self.parent[x] = self.find(self.parent[x])
        return self.parent[x]
    
    def union(self, x, y):
        xr = self.find(x)
        yr = self.find(y)
        if xr != yr:
            self.parent[yr] = xr

n, m = map(int, input().split())
matrix = [input().strip() for _ in range(n)]

dsu = DSU(n + m)

for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if matrix[i][j] == '=':
            dsu.union(i, n + j)

valid = True
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        root_i = dsu.find(i)
        root_bj = dsu.find(n + j)
        if root_i == root_bj and matrix[i][j] != '=':
            print("No")
            exit()

group_roots = set(dsu.find(u) for u in range(n + m))
group_list = list(group_roots)

from collections import defaultdict
adj = defaultdict(list)

for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        a_ij = matrix[i][j]
        if a_ij == '=':
            continue
        root_a = dsu.find(i)
        root_b = dsu.find(n + j)
        if a_ij == '>':
            adj[root_b].append(root_a)
        else:
            adj[root_a].append(root_b)

in_degree = defaultdict(int)
for u in adj:
    for v in adj[u]:
        in_degree[v] += 1

from collections import deque
queue = deque()
for u in group_list:
    if in_degree.get(u, 0) == 0:
        queue.append(u)

topo_order = []
while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    topo_order.append(u)
    for v in adj[u]:
        in_degree[v] -= 1
        if in_degree[v] == 0:
            queue.append(v)

if len(topo_order) != len(group_list):
    print("No")
    exit()

levels = {u: 1 for u in group_list}
for u in topo_order:
    for v in adj[u]:
        if levels[v] < levels[u] + 1:
            levels[v] = levels[u] + 1

a_values = [levels[dsu.find(i)] for i in range(n)]
b_values = [levels[dsu.find(n + j)] for j in range(m)]

for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        a_val = a_values[i]
        b_val = b_values[j]
        c = matrix[i][j]
        if c == '=' and a_val != b_val:
            valid = False
        elif c == '>' and a_val <= b_val:
            valid = False
        elif c == '<' and a_val >= b_val:
            valid = False

if not valid:
    print("No")
else:
    print("Yes")
    print(' '.join(map(str, a_values)))
    print(' '.join(map(str, b_values)))<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

Question: as of december 312016 what was the ratio of the approximate number of residential vehicles to the large-container industrial
Answer:

First, let's figure out the numbers we're dealing with. According to the data from the end of 2016, there were about 7,300 residential vehicles and 4,100 large-container industrial vehicles. So, if we want to find the ratio between these two groups of vehicles, we start by simply writing it as 7,300 to 4,100.

Now, I think it makes more sense if we simplify this ratio. Ratios are easier to interpret when they’re simplified, right? To do that, we should find a number that can divide both 7,300 and 4,100 evenly. Let's see, they both seem to end in two zeros, so without thinking too much, dividing by 100 seems like a good first step.

Dividing both 7,300 and 4,100 by 100 gives us 73 to 41. That was neat! So now we have a cleaner, simplified ratio: 73:41.

But just to be double sure, let’s verify this by using the Euclidean algorithm, which is a handy way to find the greatest common divisor (GCD). We’re really just confirming here, so let's go through it:

When 7,300 is divided by 4,100, it leaves a remainder of 3,200. Then, divide 4,100 by 3,200 and get a remainder of 900. What’s next? 3,200 divided by 900 leaves 500. Continuing, 900 by 500 leaves 400. Then 500 by 400 leaves a remainder of 100. Finally, dividing 400 by 100 gives a remainder of 0. Oh, awesome, that means 100 is indeed the GCD.

So yeah, dividing the original numbers, 7,300 and 4,100, by 100—just what we did initially—gives us this simplified 73 to 41 ratio. It feels good to verify that math!

Now, let's also think about this ratio in another way. What if we expressed it as a decimal instead? Dividing 7,300 by 4,100 gives us around 1.78049. This decimal lets us see the ratio more intuitively, in terms of how many residential vehicles there are per large-container industrial vehicle.

Yet, the simplified ratio of 73:41 matches the analysis pretty well, showing that for every 73 residential vehicles, there are 41 large-container industrial ones.

Ultimately, the process of both simplifying and verifying with different methods confirms the ratio nicely, ending at 73:41 as of December 31, 2016.<#sep#>In triangle \( \triangle ABC \) with \( \angle B = 120^\circ \), there is a point \( D \) on segment \( AC \) such that \( \angle DCB = 2 \angle DBC \) and \( AD = BC \). <#sep#>user: What are some important topics within AI safety. For example, if general AI is achieved, how can humans make sure that the AI will not just achieve its goals at any cost, disregarding human values and safety. How can AI be leveraged to achieve productive technological progress safely and without the risk of AI causing harm to humans or the environment of Earth?

assistant: AI safety is a rapidly developing field that aims to address the risks and challenges associated with developing advanced artificial intelligence (AI) systems. Some of the key topics within AI safety include:

1. Value alignment: Ensuring that an AI system's objectives are aligned with human values and goals, so that the system acts in a way that is beneficial to humanity.

2. Robustness: Ensuring that AI systems are resilient to adversarial attacks and maintain safe and reliable behavior even in the face of unexpected circumstances.

3. Explainability and interpretability: Ensuring that AI systems are transparent and explainable, so that their behavior can be understood and audited by human experts.

4. Control and governance: Ensuring that humans remain in control of AI systems, and that appropriate mechanisms are in place to ensure their safe and responsible use.

5. Ethics and social impact: Considering the ethical and social implications of AI systems, and ensuring that they are developed and deployed in a way that is consistent with human values and rights.

6. Alignment and coordination among AI systems: Ensuring that multiple AI systems, including those developed by different organizations or with different objectives, can work together in a safe and coordinated manner.

7. Risk assessment and management: Evaluating and mitigating the risks associated with AI systems, including the potential for unintended consequences and catastrophic failure.

8. Human-AI collaboration: Developing AI systems that can effectively collaborate with humans and augment their abilities, while minimizing the risk of harm.

These are just some of the many important topics within AI safety, and the field continues to evolve as AI technology advances and new challenges emerge.

user: I'm concerned about number 6 Alignment and coordination among AI systems: Ensuring that multiple AI systems, including those developed by different organizations or with different objectives, can work together in a safe and coordinated manner. 

Define a safe and coordinated manner to this approach?

assistant: When we talk about alignment and coordination among AI systems, we're essentially talking about how different AI systems can work together in a way that benefits humans and doesn't cause any harm. To do this, we need to make sure that the objectives of each AI system are aligned with human values and goals. 

Imagine a group of people are working on a project. These people all have different aspirations and skillsets and often speak different languages, but they're able to coordinate and work together to achieve a common goal. Communication, oversight and transparency are critical for a project to come to life. 

Similarly different AI systems will have different objectives and specializations and come from different organizations. For these systems to interact in a predictable and safe way, there must be communication, oversight and transparency between them.<#sep#>-

MOD = 998244353

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
w = list(map(int, input().split()))
current = [x % MOD for x in w]

for _ in range(m):
    s = sum(current) % MOD
    inv_s = pow(s, MOD-2, MOD)
    delta = [1 if ai else -1 for ai in a]
    new_current = []
    for i in range(n):
        term = current[i] * delta[i] % MOD
        term = term * inv_s % MOD
        new_val = (current[i] + term) % MOD
        new_current.append(new_val)
    current = new_current

for val in current:
    print(val % MOD)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

tiles = input().split()

def is_mentsu(tiles):
    if all(t == tiles[0] for t in tiles):
        return True
    suit = tiles[0][1]
    for t in tiles[1:]:
        if t[1] != suit:
            return False
    nums = sorted(int(t[0]) for t in tiles)
    return nums[0] + 1 == nums[1] and nums[1] + 1 == nums[2]

if is_mentsu(tiles):
    print(0)
else:
    from collections import defaultdict
    count = defaultdict(int)
    for t in tiles:
        count[t] += 1

    min_triplet = 3
    for s in ['m', 'p', 's']:
        for num in range(1, 10):
            tile = f"{num}{s}"
            current = count.get(tile, 0)
            needed = 3 - current
            if needed < min_triplet:
                min_triplet = needed

    min_sequence = 3
    for s in ['m', 'p', 's']:
        for start in range(1, 8):
            seq_tiles = [f"{start}{s}", f"{start+1}{s}", f"{start+2}{s}"]
            needed = 0
            for t in seq_tiles:
                needed += max(0, 1 - count.get(t, 0))
            if needed < min_sequence:
                min_sequence = needed

    print(min(min_triplet, min_sequence))<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

- Now add leaf 4, query with the two closest pair so far. E.g., if current pair is 1,2, we can query leaves 1,2,4. The result tells us if 4 should be paired with 1 or 2, or with the third.

Wait, perhaps for each new leaf, we insert it into the structure step by step.

Alternatively, let's think as if we're building an unrooted tree and later root it. 

Alternatively, maybe the following approach can work:

We can select a set of nodes in such a way that their LCAs can give us information about the tree structure.

Another Idea inspired by the LCA.

We can use the notion of Most Recent Common Ancestor (MRCA). 

Suppose that in a triple (a, b, c), the pair with the deepest LCA (i.e., the MRCA with the highest depth / furthest away from root). Let's say the deepest is between b and c. Then, this suggests that in the tree, the pathway to a splits first. 

Therefore, the group of leaves can be partitioned into the pair bc (closer to each other), and the third node is in a different subtree.

Perhaps the way to use this is to start building clusters. 

The algorithm could be like:

Initialize each leaf as its own cluster. 

While there are multiple clusters:

Choose three clusters (any three, perhaps.), form a triple by picking a leaf from each.

The query gives information about their pairwise LCA depths, indicating which pair is closer. 

Merge the two clusters of the pair with greatest depth's LCA. 

This seems like a hierarchical clustering process, repeating until the final two clusters are merged.

Wait, clusters being grouped into pairs based on their pairs' LCA being the deepest in the triplet.

But how do we choose cluster representatives? Since clusters are groups of leaves, each representative can be any leaf from the cluster's leaves. So, to ask a question among the different clusters, we need one leaf from each to form the triple. 

Hmm, this seems promising. 

Let me try to detail it:

Each cluster is a set of leaves. The algorithm proceeds as follows:

1. Initialize each leaf as a cluster. 

2. While the number of clusters is greater than 1:

   a. Select three clusters A, B, C.

   b. Choose a leaf from each of A, B, C (say a in A, b in B, c in C).

   c. Query a, b, c.

   d. Let the deepest LCA be between the pair with the deepest LCA among (a,b), (b,c), (c,a). 

   e. Suppose that between pair (a, b) is the deepest. This means that the two clusters A and B should merge first.

   f. Merge clusters A and B into a single cluster.

Repeat until all leaves are in one cluster.

At the end, the merging history forms the tree, with each merge representing a parent node. 

Wait, this is similar to Unweighted Pair Group Method with Arithmetic Mean (UPGMA) or neighbor joining methods in phylogenetics. 

Yes, perhaps this hierarchical clustering approach can be used. 

Now the key is to choose which three clusters to pick each time to form the triple. The selection must be done efficiently without exceeding the query limits.

However, in the worst case, this may require O(n^2) steps, but since n is up to 1000, and queries are limited to 10n ~10e4, this approach may not be feasible unless optimized.

Alternatively, perhaps merging clusters in a way that each merge is done with minimal queries. 

Wait each merge after the first step reduces the number of clusters by one. Since starting at n clusters and ending with 1, the process requires n-1 merges, each step requiring a single query. So, this approach would require OF(n) queries, which would fit within 10n. 

Yes, that makes sense. 

Wait let's see: 

Suppose the number of merges needed is (n-1), because each merge reduces the clusters count by 1, and the process starts with n and ends with 1. Thus, that would be n-1 merges. Each merge requires 1 query. Thus total of (n-1) queries. Which is within the 10n limit.

Wait this is brilliant!

Initialize clusters each as leaf nodes. 

While there are at least two clusters:

Alternatively, perhaps choosing clusters in a way that allows to pick any three clusters that are still separate, until no more clusters remain.

Тогда цель будет достойной, а путь к ней никогда не будет скучным.

Alternatively, when two clusters remain, they must merge as siblings. 

So the algorithm can proceed as follows:

While the number of clusters is more than 1:

   If there are exactly two clusters left:

       Merge the two. Since they must form two children of the next higher node. 

       The above step can be done without a query. 

   Else:

       pick three clusters, make a query, merge the two forming the deepest pair. 

Wait but how does that work?

Wait suppose three clusters: A, B, C. 

Upon querying a triplet with their representatives, if the deepest pair is between A and B, then you merge them into cluster AB, and proceed. 

The merging decisions are guided by the parent nodes, representing the grouping. 

As long as we always merge the two clusters indicated as closest, the final tree should reflect the true structure. 

Now, the number of queries is: the number of merges minus one? Or for every merge except those last two ?

Wait each merge from 3 or more clusters requires a query. 

The total number of queries would be the total merges minus "the times saved when merging the last two".

Let me count:

Total merges needed for n clusters: n-1. 

If all but the last two are done with one query each,

The first n-3 merges (assuming n clusters to 2 clusters) require (n-3) queries. 

Then the last merge, between two clusters, costs 0.

Total: (n-3) +0 = n-3. 

Wait but when merging to get down from 3 clusters to 2: 

Initially when clusters are 3, the process requires a query, so that's 2 merges and 1 query. 

Wait perhaps it's better to think step by step:

n clusters:

To reduce the clusters from k to k-1, each step must choose three, then merge two. Thus for each step where k ≥3:

1 query. 

Thus, total queries are (starting from k = n) until we reach k=2. 

Thus, from n to 2 is (n-2) steps, taking (n-2) queries. 

The last step merges 2 clusters with no query needed, but that step is not counted because it's after queries. 

Hence, total queries is (n-2). 

Since n is up to 1000, (n-2) is under 1000, which is within the 10n (10,000) limit. So this approach is feasible.

Now the steps to implement:

First, the algorithm needs to select three cluster representatives each time, run the query, and merge the clusters accordingly. 

The first thing needed is an efficient data structure for the clusters. Let's think in terms of objects or arrays.

Each cluster can be represented by:

- a list of leaves it contains. 

- possibly some identifying information. 

However, for efficiency, perhaps when merging, when given three clusters, we just track each as a group.

The key steps for each iteration:

1. Pick three clusters among the remaining clusters. 

Which ones? To optimize, we can maybe choose one cluster and two others, but any choice should work because the query will guide merging. 

Alternatively, the selection of the three clusters can be arbitrary, as long as there are three to pick. 

So the process is:

Pick any three distinct clusters (A, B, C), then pick one leaf from each (A can contribute any of its leaves). 

Then perform query, get the answer, which tells us which of the three pairs has the deepest LCA. 

We then merge those two clusters, and proceed. 

Now, the representation of clusters can be handled with a list of cluster objects, perhaps as a list of current clusters. 

Implementation Steps:

Initially, each cluster is a singleton list (each leaf is in its own cluster).

clusters = list of [ [1], [2], ... [n] ]

loop:

   while the number of clusters > 1:

       if current clusters have exactly two left:

            merge them, (they need to be siblings under a new parent node)

            break.

       else:

           pick three distinct clusters from the list, say c1, c2, c3. 

           choose any leaf from each: a from c1, b from c2, c from c3.

           ask query a, b, c.

           determine which pair (a and b, etc.) has the deepest LCA.

           if the deepest pair is a and b (i.e., the first pair):

               merge clusters c1 and c2.

           elif it's b and c:

               merge c2 and c3.

           else (a with c): merge c1 and c3.

           repeat until only one cluster remains.

However, once merged, the clusters are updated.

This process would require O(N) steps, each step requiring O(1) time except for finding the three clusters, but that can be handled by iterating through a list. 

But, the problem is selecting three clusters each time. For the first iterations, we can pick first three clusters in an array. 

Now, after building the clusters through merges, how to reconstruct the tree structure. 

The merging process must be recorded so that we can build the tree from the bottom up. 

Hmm, the clusters can be represented with parent pointers. Each time we merge clusters, we can create a new node and set its parents as the two merged clusters. 

Wait, to make this explicit:

Each cluster must store its root and perhaps links between the nodes. 

Alternatively, think of each cluster as a node in the cluster hiearchy. Every time two clusters merge, create a new node as their parent, and set the two clusters as children.

Initially, all leaves are singletons (each represented by their leaf's identifier in some way).

When two clusters are merged, for bookkeeping, we can create a new parent node, and track the structure.

Wait, since the final answer requires us to output the parent for each node, we need to track the nodes of the tree we are builing.

So perhaps the process should be augmented to record the tree structure.

Alternative implementation idea:

We can track an explicit tree structure, with nodes being either original leaf nodes or internal nodes created during merges.

Each merge between clusters and creates an internal parent node that connects the two merged clusters. 

Let me think:

Let each cluster object contain:

- a list of leaves.

- a pointer to its corresponding root node in the constructed tree. 

When two cluster A and B are merged:

- a new node (say, numbered as next_node) is created.

- This node becomes the parent of the previous roots of clusters A and B. 

- The new cluster’s list is all leaves from A and B. 

- The root of the new cluster is the new node (next_node). 

This way, the tree grows in the parent pointers as we merge.

Starting with each leaf as its own node (so node i represents the leaf, holding its leaves in the cluster's list as a singleton [i], and its root is itself).

When merging two clusters, a new parent node is created, taking the next available node number, and set its children as the roots of each cluster. 

Thus, this builds the tree incrementally as we merge clusters guided by the queries.<#sep#>Renaming `iotedgedev create` to `iotedgedev new`
I am thinking about renaming the command `iotedgedev create` to `iotedgedev new` for the sake of simplicity and aligning with common practices (as how the command to create new projects is called in VS and VS Code). @jongio, do you think this is a good idea?
